  ⎿  Created CLAUDE.md (+27 -0)
       1 +# ID Generation Service — instructions for Claude Code         
       2 +                                                               
       3 +The full spec is **[docs/architecture.md](docs/architecture.md)
         +**. Read it before any change.                                 
       4 +The API contract, error codes, counter scoping (ADR-003) and al
         +location design (ADR-001) there are binding.                   
       5 +                                                               
       6 +## Rules                                                       
       7 +- Java 21, Quarkus 3.x, Maven wrapper (`./mvnw`), one module, p
         +ackage `org.egov.idgen`.                                       
       8 +- Extensions: `rest`, `rest-jackson`, `reactive-pg-client`, `sm
         +allrye-health`, `hibernate-validator`, `config-yaml`. **No ORM,
         + no tables** — Postgres holds only sequences.                  
       9 +- **Never modify anything in `data/`.** It must stay byte-for-b
         +yte as provided.                                               
      10 +- Follow the API contract and error codes in the spec exactly. 
         +DTOs are Java `record`s.                                       
      11 +- Locks are per counter key, never global. Never call `nextval`
         + per ID — always go through `BlockAllocator`. If endpoints run 
         +on virtual threads, use `ReentrantLock` instead of `synchronize
         +d`.                                                            
      12 +- Block size comes from `pg_sequences.increment_by`, never from
         + app config.                                                   
      13 +- Every step ends with `./mvnw test` green and `docker compose 
         +up --build` working. Commit after each step with a clear messag
         +e.                                                             
      14 +- When unsure, choose the option that **cannot produce a duplic
         +ate**, and add a line to `NOTES.md`.                           
      15 +                                                               
      16 +## Commands                                                    
      17 +- Tests: `./mvnw test` (needs Docker: Dev Services starts Postg
         +res)                                                           
      18 +- Dev mode: `./mvnw quarkus:dev`                               
      19 +- Full stack: `docker compose up --build` → http://localhost:80
         +80                                                             
      20 +- Load test: `docker compose --profile test run --rm loadtest` 
      21 +                                                               
      22 +## Layout                                                      
      23 +- `src/main/java/org/egov/idgen/{config,template,sequence,issue
         +,recent,api}` — see "Code structure" in the spec               
      24 +- `src/main/resources/META-INF/resources/index.html` — static U
         +I                                                              
      25 +- `nginx/nginx.conf` — round-robin to 3 backend replicas       
      26 +- `loadtest/loadtest.py` — multiprocess duplicate check        
      27 +- `docs/adr/` — ADRs 0001–0003

  Ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

● I've scaffolded Quarkus and finished step 1 of your build plan (the skeleton).
  Its done-check passes: docker compose up serves /api/ping through Nginx on
  :8080, requests rotate across the 3 backend containers, and /q/health/ready
  returns UP with the Postgres check UP. ./mvnw test also passes (2 tests).

  What's in the repo now
  - Quarkus project: Quarkus 3.40.1, Java 21, with the Maven wrapper and the
    extensions your plan lists, plus config-yaml so settings can live in
    application.yaml. Package is org.egov.idgen.
  - PingResource.java (src/main/java/org/egov/idgen/api/PingResource.java): GET
    /api/ping returns the hostname of the instance that answered, so you can see
    the load balancing. PingResourceTest.java
    (src/test/java/org/egov/idgen/api/PingResourceTest.java) covers it and the
    readiness check.
  - application.yaml (src/main/resources/application.yaml): the idgen.* settings
    from your doc. In compose it connects to the compose Postgres; tests and
    dev mode start a throwaway Postgres in Docker automatically.
  - Dockerfile: two stages, a JDK 21 build and a JRE runtime, with a healthcheck
    on /q/health/ready.
  - docker-compose.yml: Postgres 16, 3 backend replicas, Nginx (the only port
    exposed on your machine, 8080), and a loadtest service you run with
    --profile test.
  - nginx/nginx.conf: round-robins across the replicas and re-checks their
    addresses every 5s, so a restarted backend rejoins the pool.

> why 21 java?

● Because your architecture doc says so: "Rules for Claude Code" requires Java
  21, Quarkus 3.x, and I followed it. It's still a reasonable choice:

  - It's a long-term support release. Your machine has JDK 26, which is a
    short-lived release (about 6 months of updates), so you wouldn't target it
    for a service.
  - It has everything the design needs: records for the DTOs, sealed interfaces
    for Token, and virtual threads.
  - Low risk on the reviewer's machine. The Docker build uses the
    eclipse-temurin:21 images, so docker compose up never depends on whatever
    JDK the reviewer has installed. Building for 21 on your JDK 26 works fine,
    as the passing tests show.

  The other option is Java 25, the newer long-term release (September 2025).
  It's arguably the more current choice for an interview, and Quarkus 3.40
  supports it. Nothing in your design needs 25-only features, so it adds a
  talking point but no function. Switching takes three changes:
  maven.compiler.release in pom.xml, the two eclipse-temurin image tags in the
  Dockerfile, and the version line in CLAUDE.md and your doc.

  I'd keep 21 unless you want 25 as a point to mention in the interview. Want me
  to switch?

  About the 502s: for about 5 seconds after the rebuild, Nginx returned 502. The
  backend containers had been recreated with new addresses, and Nginx served
  "no live upstreams" until its next lookup, then recovered without help. That
  gap matters for the "restart a backend mid-test" check. A restarted replica
  keeps its address, so a plain restart shouldn't hit it. If the load test does
  see 502s, a short retry in the test client is the fix.

      383 +## ADR-004: Nginx as the load balancer, not an API gateway    
      384 +                                                              
      385 +**Decision:** a plain Nginx reverse proxy is the only public e
          +ntry point. It round-robins across the backend replicas and do
          +es nothing else. No API gateway (Kong, Spring Cloud Gateway, a
          + cloud API gateway). Accepted, 13 Nov 2026.                   
      386 +                                                              
      387 +**Context.** The POC needs exactly two things in front of the 
          +backend: one public port, so reviewers' requests spread over 3
          + instances (ADR-001's multi-instance proof), and a way to keep
          + serving while one instance restarts. What an API gateway adds
          + (authentication, API keys, per-tenant rate limits, request tr
          +ansformation, a developer portal) is all a non-goal here: ther
          +e is no auth in scope, and the API is two `POST`s and three `G
          +ET`s.                                                         
      388 +                                                              
      389 +| Option | What it brings | Cost here | Verdict |             
      390 +|---|---|---|---|                                             
      391 +| Nginx reverse proxy (chosen) | Round-robin, failover to the 
          +next instance, re-resolving replica addresses | One container,
          + about 20 lines of config | Does exactly the job, nothing to l
          +earn or configure |                                           
      392 +| Kong / Tyk | Auth, rate limits, keys, plugins | Its own DB o
          +r declarative config; plugins we would not turn on | Right onc
          +e auth arrives, not before |                                  
      393 +| Spring Cloud Gateway | Routing and filters in Java | A secon
          +d JVM app to build, test and start | More code than the servic
          +e it fronts |                                                 
      394 +| Traefik | Discovers containers by Docker labels | Mounts the
          + Docker socket into a public container | Works, but adds a sec
          +urity trade-off for no gain |                                 
      395 +| Envoy | Rich load balancing, active health checks | Verbose 
          +config, harder to read in a review | Overkill for 3 replicas |
      396 +| Cloud API gateway (AWS, Azure, GCP) | Managed auth, quotas, 
          +TLS | Can't run in `docker compose up` on a reviewer's laptop 
          +| Breaks the one-command rule |                               
      397 +                                                              
      398 +**Consequences**                                              
      399 +- **No edge protection:** any caller can issue IDs for any ten
          +ant, with no rate limit. Acceptable because authentication is 
          +out of scope (ADR-003 already lists it as "another day").     
      400 +- **Passive health only:** open-source Nginx removes an instan
          +ce only after a request to it fails, then retries on the next 
          +one. A `POST` that already reached an instance is not retried,
          + so a retry never issues IDs twice by accident (it would only 
          +burn numbers, but staying predictable matters more).          
      401 +- **Re-resolution gap:** when replicas are recreated with new 
          +addresses, Nginx can return 502 for up to 5 seconds until it r
          +e-resolves `backend`. A plain restart keeps the address and is
          + not affected.                                                
      402 +- **Nothing in the app depends on Nginx.** Replacing it with a
          + gateway or a Kubernetes Ingress later is a deployment change,
          + not a code change.                                           
      403 +

          +uthenticate.**                                                
      405 +- In production this service would sit behind the platform's e
          +xisting gateway, which already does authentication and routing
          + for the other city services.                                 
      406 +- The gateway verifies the caller's token and passes the tenan
          +t as a trusted header or claim; the ID service stops trusting 
          +`city` from the request body (ADR-003).

      408 +- In Kubernetes, Nginx is replaced by a Service plus an Ingres
          +s (or the gateway itself), with readiness from `/q/health/read

● I've written ADR-004, "Nginx as the load balancer, not an API gateway". It's
  in your Claude Doc right after ADR-003, and in the repo as
  docs/adr/0004-nginx-over-api-gateway.md.

  What it argues:
  - The decision: a plain Nginx proxy is the only public entry point. All it
    does is spread requests round-robin across the 3 backends.
  - Why: the POC needs one public port and a way to keep serving through a
    restart. Everything an API gateway adds (auth, API keys, rate limits,
    request transformation) is out of scope.
  - Options compared in a table: Nginx, Kong/Tyk, Spring Cloud Gateway, Traefik,
    Envoy, and cloud API gateways. Each gets a one-line reason it loses here.
    For example, a cloud gateway can't run inside docker compose up, and Traefik
    needs the Docker socket mounted into a public container.
  - Consequences:
    - No auth or rate limits at the edge.
    - An instance is only dropped after a request to it fails. A POST that
      already reached an instance is never retried.
    - There's a 502 window of up to 5 seconds when replicas are recreated, which
      we saw in step 1.
    - The app doesn't depend on Nginx, so replacing it is a deployment change,
      not a code change.
  - Another day: run behind the platform's existing gateway once tenants
    authenticate. The gateway verifies the token and passes the tenant, and
    per-tenant rate limits protect narrow counters like SEQ_WS:4. In Kubernetes,
    Nginx becomes an Ingress.

  One claim to check: the ADR says a production deployment would sit behind "the
  platform's existing gateway". I believe DIGIT has one, but I haven't
  confirmed it, so I didn't name it. If you know which one it is, naming it
  would land well in the interview.

  Updated to match:
  - In the doc and docs/architecture.md, "docs/adr/0001–0003" is now
    "0001–0004", and the repo layout lists the new file.
  - CLAUDE.md now says ADRs 0001–0004.
  - docs/ai-log.md and docs/NOTES.md each have a line about the new ADR.
  - REMINDERS.md has the Nginx decision in the email's decisions list.

  docs/adr/ only has 0004 for now. Per the plan, 0001–0003 get written at the
  7:00 documents step, but I can create them from the doc now so the folder is
  complete. Still nothing committed.

● Bash(python -I - <<'EOF'
      import re…)
  ⎿  Created docs\adr\0001-sequences-in-memory-blocks.md (+48 -0)
       1 +## ADR-001: Postgres sequences with in-memory blocks, no tables
       2 +                                                               
       3 +**Decision:** each counter key gets one Postgres sequence with 
         +`INCREMENT BY 100`. A backend instance calls `nextval` once, ta
         +kes the 100 numbers starting there, and issues them from memory
         +. The service has no tables. Accepted, 13 Nov 2026.            
       4 +                                                               
       5 +**Context.** If every ID needs a DB round trip, busy keys becom
         +e a bottleneck. The brief allows gaps but never a duplicate, an
         +d that is how Postgres sequences behave: `nextval` never blocks
         + other callers, never rolls back, and never returns the same va
         +lue twice. You can get gaps, but never duplicates.             
       6 +                                                               
       7 +**How it works**                                               
       8 +1. **One sequence per counter key:** (template, city, period) f
         +or `[SEQ:n]`; (named counter, city, period) for `[SEQ_NAME:n]`.
         + Templates with no `[CITY]` in the output (`PGR-`) get one glob
         +al sequence. Names follow `seq_<counter>_<city>_<period>` in lo
         +wer case, within Postgres's 63-character limit.                
       9 +1. **Created at startup** for the current and next period, `STA
         +RT WITH` the configured `start` (1 by default). If another inst
         +ance creates it at the same moment, catch `duplicate_object` an
         +d carry on.                                                    
      10 +1. **In memory:** `{next, end}` per key, guarded by a per-key l
         +ock. When `next == end`, one `nextval` returns `base`, and the 
         +block is `[base, base+100)`. Only one refill per key runs at a 
         +time; other callers wait for it.                               
      11 +1. **Bulk:** take what's left in the block. If more are needed,
         + fetch several blocks in one trip with `SELECT nextval(…) FROM 
         +generate_series(1, k)`.                                        
      12 +1. **Recent-IDs view:** an in-memory list of the last 50 IDs pe
         +r (template, city) on each instance.                           
      13 +                                                               
      14 +```sql                                                         
      15 +CREATE SEQUENCE IF NOT EXISTS seq_pg_receipt_amr_2026_27 START 
         +WITH 1    INCREMENT BY 100;                                    
      16 +CREATE SEQUENCE IF NOT EXISTS seq_ws_amr                 START 
         +WITH 1000 INCREMENT BY 10;                                     
      17 +CREATE SEQUENCE IF NOT EXISTS seq_pgr_complaint          START 
         +WITH 1    INCREMENT BY 100;                                    
      18 +                                                               
      19 +SELECT nextval('seq_pg_receipt_amr_2026_27');                  
         +     -- one block                                              
      20 +SELECT nextval('seq_pg_receipt_amr_2026_27') FROM generate_seri
         +es(1, 5);  -- five blocks, one trip                            
      21 +```                                                            
      22 +                                                               
      23 +**Why it can't duplicate.** Each `nextval` returns a different 
         +starting number, `INCREMENT` apart, so blocks never overlap, wh
         +ether across instances, restarts or crashes. The in-memory lock
         + only protects a block that this one instance already owns.    
      24 +                                                               
      25 +**Why no tables at all.** The only state that has to be durable
         + and shared is "which numbers are taken", and a sequence holds 
         +exactly that. Templates and cities are configuration loaded fro
         +m `data/` at startup, not data the service owns. The recent-IDs
         + view is a convenience and can live in memory. Any table would 
         +either duplicate what the sequence already guarantees (a counte
         +rs table) or put a write back on every request (an issued-IDs l
         +og). If an audit trail is needed later, add an `issued_ids` tab
         +le written in batches off the request path. That is an additive
         + change and doesn't affect how numbers are allocated.          
      26 +                                                               
      27 +| Option | DB trips per ID | Tables | Verdict |                
      28 +|---|---|---|---|                                              
      29 +| Counters table, `UPDATE` per request | 1 | 1 | Hot row lock; 
         +most moving parts |                                            
      30 +| Counters table, hi-lo block | 1/100 | 1 | Works, but rebuilds
         + what a sequence already is |                                  
      31 +| Sequence with `CACHE 100` | 1 | 0 | Caches inside each Postgr
         +es connection; the app still makes a trip per `nextval` |      
      32 +| **Sequence ****`INCREMENT BY 100`**** + app block (chosen)** 
         +| 1/100 | 0 | Fewest trips, no locks in the DB, nothing to main
         +tain |                                                         
      33 +                                                               
      34 +**Consequences**                                               
      35 +- **Gaps:** an instance restart or a new period leaves up to 99
         + unused numbers per key per instance. The brief allows this.   
      36 +- **Order:** IDs are unique but not in strict time order across
         + instances (A issues 1–100 while B issues 101–200).            
      37 +- **Block size must come from the sequence:** at startup the ap
         +p reads `increment_by` from `pg_sequences`, not from its own co
         +nfig. If an instance assumed 500 while the sequence increments 
         +by 100, its blocks would overlap.                              
      38 +- **Changing block size:** raising it (`ALTER SEQUENCE … INCREM
         +ENT BY 500`) is safe. Lowering it is not: first `setval` past t
         +he highest block already handed out, or blocks will overlap.   
      39 +- **Narrow counters:** `SEQ_WS:4` has only 9,000 values, so it 
         +uses `INCREMENT BY 10`.                                        
      40 +- **No issued-ID log:** there's no audit trail and no DB-level 
         +unique key as a backup. Uniqueness rests on the sequence plus c
         +orrect rendering, and the load test is what proves it. The rece
         +nt-IDs view is per instance and resets on restart. Audit histor
         +y is out of scope.                                             
     … 8 more lines (ctrl+o to expand)
  ⎿  Created docs\adr\0002-quarkus.md (+29 -0)
       1 +## ADR-002: Build the service on Quarkus (JVM now, native later
         +)                                                              
       2 +                                                               
       3 +**Decision:** the backend is a Quarkus app, run in JVM mode for
         + this submission. Native image is the first item for "another d
         +ay". Accepted, 13 Nov 2026.                                    
       4 +                                                               
       5 +**Context.** The ID service is stateless apart from its in-memo
         +ry blocks (ADR-001), so it scales by adding pods. How useful th
         +at is depends on how fast a new pod is ready to serve: slow sta
         +rtup means a traffic spike is over before the new pods arrive. 
         +Quarkus does most framework wiring at build time rather than at
         + startup, so it starts fast and uses little memory.            
       6 +                                                               
       7 +**Why JVM mode for the ****3****-hour POC**                    
       8 +- Startup is typically about a second in JVM mode. Millisecond 
         +startup comes from **native** mode, which is roughly tens of ms
         + (approximate, from Quarkus's published figures).              
       9 +- A native build takes several minutes and needs several GB of 
         +RAM for the GraalVM/Mandrel builder. If that happened inside `d
         +ocker compose up` on a reviewer's laptop, the build itself woul
         +d be the biggest risk.                                         
      10 +- The code is the same either way, so going native later is a b
         +uild flag (`-Dnative`), not a rewrite.                         
      11 +                                                               
      12 +**Shape of the app**                                           
      13 +- **REST:** `quarkus-rest` with `POST /api/ids` (single) and `P
         +OST /api/ids/bulk`, plus one exception mapper for the error for
         +mat.                                                           
      14 +- **Postgres:** `quarkus-reactive-pg-client` (or Agroal JDBC) c
         +alling only `CREATE SEQUENCE` at startup and `nextval` on refil
         +l. No Hibernate/ORM, since there are no tables.                
      15 +- **Blocks:** `ConcurrentHashMap<String, Block>` with one lock 
         +per key. Single issues take the next value; bulk issues take a 
         +range in one go.                                               
      16 +- **Config:** `templates.json` and `cities.csv` are read once a
         +t startup (`@Startup`). A config error fails startup with a cle
         +ar message.                                                    
      17 +- **UI:** a static HTML/JS page served from `META-INF/resources
         +`, so there's no separate frontend container.                  
      18 +- **Health:** `quarkus-smallrye-health` (`/q/health/ready`) fee
         +ds the compose healthcheck and, later, the Kubernetes readiness
         + probe.                                                        
      19 +                                                               
      20 +**Consequences**                                               
      21 +- **Scaling interacts with ADR-001:** every new pod takes fresh
         + blocks, and every pod removed abandons its unused ones. A sequ
         +ence can't take numbers back, so frequent scale-up and scale-do
         +wn means more gaps. Uniqueness is unaffected; larger gaps is th
         +e cost of elasticity, and it's within the brief.               
      22 +- **Pod readiness is more than startup time:** image pull, sche
         +duling and the first DB connection add seconds. Native shrinks 
         +the app's share of that, and a small native image also pulls fa
         +ster.                                                          
      23 +- **Reviewers need nothing extra:** the Dockerfile does a multi
         +-stage Maven build, so `docker compose up` is still the only co
         +mmand. The first build downloads dependencies and takes a few m
         +inutes; say so in the README.                                  
      24 +                                                               
      25 +**With another day: prove elastic scaling with native**        
      26 +1. Build the native image (`./mvnw package -Dnative -Dquarkus.n
         +ative.container-build=true`) and record its startup time and RS
         +S against JVM mode.                                            
      27 +1. Deploy to a local Kubernetes cluster (kind/k3d) with a Horiz
         +ontalPodAutoscaler on CPU or request rate.                     
      28 +1. Run the concurrency test as a ramping load: scale from 1 to 
         +N pods and back during the run.                                
      29 +1. Report the time from scale-out decision to a ready pod, and 
         +that the full ID set still has zero duplicates across every pod
         + that came and went.                                           
  ⎿  Created docs\adr\0003-counter-scoping-multi-tenancy.md (+54 -0)
       1 +## ADR-003: Counter scoping and multi-tenancy                  
       2 +                                                               
       3 +**Decision:** tenancy is a namespace in the sequence name, insi
         +de one shared Postgres database. A counter is scoped by exactly
         + the variable parts that appear in the ID it produces, so `PGR-
         +` gets one global counter while every other template gets one p
         +er city. Accepted, 13 Nov 2026.                                
       4 +                                                               
       5 +**Context.** The brief scopes `[SEQ:n]` per (template, city) an
         +d named counters per city. Applied literally, that gives a dupl
         +icate for `PGR-[SEQ:8]`, whose output has no city. The email al
         +so asks how the design handles multi-tenancy.                  
       6 +                                                               
       7 +**The scoping rule:** a part of the key that isn't printed in t
         +he ID can't be used to tell IDs apart, so it must not scope the
         + counter.                                                      
       8 +                                                               
       9 +| Template shape | Counter key | Example sequence |            
      10 +|---|---|---|                                                  
      11 +| `[SEQ:n]` with `[CITY]` | template + city (+ period if printe
         +d) | `seq_pg_receipt_amr_2026_27` |                            
      12 +| `[SEQ:n]` without `[CITY]` | template only (+ period if print
         +ed) | `seq_pgr_complaint` |                                    
      13 +| `[SEQ_NAME:n]` with `[CITY]` | named counter + city (+ period
         + if printed); shared by every template that uses it | `seq_ws_a
         +mr` |                                                          
      14 +| `[SEQ_NAME:n]` with no `counters` entry | rejected: 422 `COUN
         +TER_NOT_CONFIGURED` | `EST_ALLOTMENT` |                        
      15 +                                                               
      16 +**Tenant resolution.** `city` accepts the tenant ID (`pb.amrits
         +ar`) or the city code (`AMR`), case-insensitively, against `cit
         +ies.csv`. An unknown value returns 404 `UNKNOWN_CITY`. The resp
         +onse echoes the canonical `tenantId` and `cityCode`.           
      17 +                                                               
      18 +**Isolation options**                                          
      19 +                                                               
      20 +| Option | Isolation | Cost | Verdict |                        
      21 +|---|---|---|---|                                              
      22 +| **Shared DB, sequences namespaced by name (chosen)** | Indepe
         +ndent numbering per tenant; no shared lock | None: a sequence h
         +olds no tenant data | Right for 10 cities and counters-only sta
         +te |                                                           
      23 +| Schema per tenant | Separate namespaces | Onboarding and migr
         +ations per tenant | Adds work with no benefit here |           
      24 +| Database per tenant/state | Hard separation, separate failure
         +s | Connection pools and operations × N | Only if a state requi
         +res data residency |                                           
      25 +                                                               
      26 +**Consequences**                                               
      27 +- **No noisy neighbours:** each tenant has its own sequences an
         +d its own in-memory lock per key, so a busy city never slows an
         +other one.                                                     
      28 +- **Onboarding a city** = a row in `cities.csv`. Its sequences 
         +are created at the next startup, or on first use.              
      29 +- **Shared fate:** one Postgres serves every tenant, so an outa
         +ge hits all of them. That's acceptable for the POC; split by st
         +ate if residency or blast radius matters.                      
      30 +- **No caller authentication:** any caller can issue IDs for an
         +y tenant. Another day: take the tenant from a verified token cl
         +aim, not the request body.                                     
      31 +                                                               
      32 +**Configuration: data files as-is, settings in ****`application
         +.yaml`****.** `cities.csv` and `templates.json` stay in `data/`
         + exactly as provided and are read at startup. They are not copi
         +ed into a table or into YAML.                                  
      33 +                                                               
      34 +| Option | Verdict |                                           
      35 +|---|---|                                                      
      36 +| **Read ****`data/`**** files at startup (chosen)** | Matches 
         +the brief ("use the provided data files as-is", "load both file
         +s at startup"). One source of truth. |                         
      37 +| Copy into `application.yaml` | A second copy of the data: a r
         +eviewer who edits `templates.json` would see no effect. |      
      38 +| Load into a `cities` / `templates` table | Brings back a tabl
         +e for data the service doesn't own (ADR-001), plus a loader to 
         +maintain. |                                                    
      39 +                                                               
      40 +`application.yaml` holds only the service's own settings, inclu
         +ding where to find the files:

● ADR-004 now has a "Configuration" part with the full Nginx config. It's in
  your Claude Doc, in docs/architecture.md and in
  docs/adr/0004-nginx-over-api-gateway.md, just before the "With another day"
  list.

  What the config does (nginx/nginx.conf):
  - resolver + server backend:8080 resolve: Docker's DNS returns one address per
    backend replica, and Nginx looks them up again every 5 seconds. A recreated
    or added replica joins the pool without restarting Nginx.
  - upstream: sends requests to the replicas in turn. Each response carries an
    X-Upstream header saying which replica answered.
  - proxy_next_upstream: if an instance can't be reached or returns 502/503, the
    request goes to the next one. A POST that already reached an instance is
    never retried, so a retry can't burn numbers or issue them twice.
  - Port 8080 is the only port published on your machine. Postgres and the
    backends stay internal.

  I also tidied the comments in the config file itself; its behaviour hasn't
  changed. After restarting Nginx it still works, and two requests in a row came
  back from different replicas (172.18.0.4 then 172.18.0.3).

  This is logged in docs/NOTES.md. Still nothing committed.

> how to test manually

● The stack is still running from earlier. In step 1 there are only two
  endpoints to try, /api/ping and /q/health/ready, so manual testing means
  checking the stack, the load balancing and failover. Run these in a terminal
  in the repo folder. In PowerShell, type curl.exe rather than curl, because
  plain curl is an alias for Invoke-WebRequest.

  1. Start the stack and check it's healthy
  docker compose up --build -d
  docker compose ps
  You should see postgres, backend-1, backend-2 and backend-3 as (healthy), and
  nginx as Up and listening on 0.0.0.0:8080.

  2. Ping through Nginx, and watch it rotate across replicas
  curl.exe -i http://localhost:8080/api/ping
  Run it a few times. The instance value in the body and the X-Upstream header
  should cycle through 3 different values.

  You can also just open http://localhost:8080/api/ping in a browser and
  refresh.

  3. Readiness, including the Postgres check
  curl.exe http://localhost:8080/q/health/ready
  Expect "status": "UP", with "Reactive PostgreSQL connections health check" UP.

  4. Failover: stop one backend
  docker stop idgen-backend-2
  curl.exe -i http://localhost:8080/api/ping     # repeat several times
  Every request should still return 200, now rotating between 2 instances. Bring
  it back and it rejoins within about 5 seconds:
  docker start idgen-backend-2

  5. Database outage: readiness goes DOWN
  docker stop idgen-postgres-1
  curl.exe http://localhost:8080/q/health/ready   # expect 503, status DOWN
  curl.exe http://localhost:8080/api/ping         # still 200: ping doesn't
  touch the DB
  docker start idgen-postgres-1

  6. Only Nginx is exposed
  curl.exe http://localhost:5432    # connection refused: Postgres isn't
  published
  docker compose port backend 8080  # prints an error: backends have no host
  port

  7. Logs, if anything looks off
  docker compose logs -f nginx backend

  Tear down with docker compose down, or docker compose down -v to wipe Postgres
  too.

  From step 3 onward there will be real endpoints to test by hand (POST
  /api/ids, /api/ids/bulk, and the error cases). Want me to add a short "Manual
  testing" section to the README now, and extend it as each step lands?

      30  Repeat a few times: `instance` in the body and the `X-Upstream`
           header cycle through 3 values.
      31  
      32  **3. Readiness includes Postgres**
      33  ```sh
      34 -curl.exe http://localhost:8080/q/health/ready                  
      34 +curl http://localhost:8080/q/health/ready                      
      35  ```
      36  Expect `"status": "UP"` with the PostgreSQL check UP.
      37  
      38  **4. Failover when a backend stops**
      39  ```sh
      40  docker stop idgen-backend-2
      41 -curl.exe -i http://localhost:8080/api/ping     # still 200, now
         - 2 instances                                                   
      41 +curl -i http://localhost:8080/api/ping     # still 200, now 2 i
         +nstances                                                       
      42  docker start idgen-backend-2                   # rejoins within
           ~5 s
      43  ```
      44  
      45  **5. Readiness goes DOWN without Postgres**
      46  ```sh
      47  docker stop idgen-postgres-1
      48 -curl.exe http://localhost:8080/q/health/ready  # 503, DOWN     
      49 -curl.exe http://localhost:8080/api/ping        # still 200 (no 
         -DB needed)                                                     
      48 +curl http://localhost:8080/q/health/ready  # 503, DOWN         
      49 +curl http://localhost:8080/api/ping        # still 200 (no DB n
         +eeded)                                                         
      50  docker start idgen-postgres-1
      51  ```
      52  
      53  **6. Only Nginx is exposed**
      54  ```sh
      55 -curl.exe http://localhost:5432                 # connection ref
         -used                                                           
      55 +curl http://localhost:5432                 # connection refused
      56  docker compose port backend 8080               # error: no host
           port
      57  ```

● I added the top 3 open questions to the email checklist in REMINDERS.md. Each
  has the POC's assumption, framed as "easy to change if you prefer otherwise":

  1. Should PGR-[SEQ:8] use one global counter, since its ID has no city?
     Assumed yes. Counting per city would give every city PGR-00000001.
  2. Should counters reset when the FY, year or month in the ID changes? Assumed
     yes, but only when that period appears in the ID.
  3. Are gaps and non-sequential order acceptable? Assumed yes. Each instance
     takes blocks of 100 numbers, so a restart can leave gaps of up to 99, and
     IDs from different instances interleave. Only uniqueness is guaranteed.

  I picked these because each one changes which IDs the system issues, and a
  reviewer could reasonably want it the other way. The first one also prevents a
  real duplicate bug.

  The other five questions (which document governs, padding overflow, timezone,
  bulk limit, recent-IDs view) stay in the full table I already linked. Tell me
  if you'd rank them differently. Padding overflow (grow vs fail) would be my
  fourth.

● Click Cancel, not Create Fork. A fork would put your code under a different
  GitHub account, which isn't what you want for the submission.

  The cause is almost certainly that VS Code is signed in to GitHub as an
  account other than mnazaar. Your commits are authored as mnazaar, but pushing
  uses whichever GitHub login the credential manager holds. Checking which login
  that is:

  Ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

● Found it: git pushes with the stored GitHub login nazaarblue, and that account
  has no write access to mnazaar/egov-poc-id-gen.

  Fix (recommended): pin this repo to the mnazaar login. Git Credential Manager
  can hold both accounts and picks one by the username in the remote URL, so
  nazaarblue keeps working for your other repos:

  git remote set-url origin
  https://mnazaar@github.com/mnazaar/egov-poc-id-gen.git
  git push origin main

  On the first push a browser window opens. Sign in to GitHub as mnazaar and
  approve. Later pushes from this repo use mnazaar automatically.

  Alternatives:
  - Make mnazaar the only stored login: run git credential-manager github logout
    nazaarblue, then push and sign in as mnazaar. The catch is that every repo
    would then push as mnazaar.
  - Give nazaarblue access: add it as a collaborator in the repo's settings on
    GitHub. Pushes would then show up as coming from nazaarblue, which looks odd
    on a repo you're submitting.

  The push opens a browser sign-in, so it's best you run it yourself. I can set
  the remote URL now if you like; it's a local change and easy to undo.

> git remote set-url origin
  https://mnazaar@github.com/mnazaar/egov-poc-id-gen.git
  git push origin main

  Ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

● The remote URL is set to the mnazaar login. The push failed because it needs
  the GitHub sign-in window, and my shell can't show interactive prompts, so the
  dialog was cancelled. Nothing was pushed and nothing is broken.

  Run the push yourself in the VS Code terminal (Ctrl+`):

  git push origin main

  A browser or popup sign-in will appear. Log in to GitHub as mnazaar (not
  nazaarblue) and approve. After that, pushes from this repo go through as
  mnazaar, and the Source Control panel's Sync button will work too.

  If no prompt appears and it fails again, run git credential-manager github
  login --username mnazaar first, then push.

> but repo is created with mnazaar

● Yes, the repo belongs to mnazaar, and that's exactly why the push fails. Git
  has three separate identities here:

  ┌───────────────┬────────────┬────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │     What      │  Account   │            Where it comes from             │
  ├───────────────┼────────────┼────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Repo owner on │ mnazaar    │ You created the repo with it               │
  │  GitHub       │            │                                            │
  ├───────────────┼────────────┼────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Commit author │ mnazaar    │ git config user.name/email: only a label   │
  │               │            │ written into each commit                   │
  ├───────────────┼────────────┼────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Login used to │ nazaarblue │ The GitHub login saved in Windows          │
  │  push         │            │ Credential Manager on this PC              │
  └───────────────┴────────────┴────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  GitHub only checks the third one. The commit author is just text and proves
  nothing. When git pushes, it sends the saved nazaarblue login, GitHub sees
  that nazaarblue has no write access to mnazaar/egov-poc-id-gen, and it
  refuses. VS Code then offers a fork, because that's the one thing nazaarblue
  is allowed to do.

  The fix is to sign in once as mnazaar for this repo. The remote URL is already
  set up for that. In the VS Code terminal:

  git push origin main

  When the sign-in window appears, log in as mnazaar. If it signs you in as
  nazaarblue automatically, log out of github.com in that browser first, or run
  this before pushing:

  git credential-manager github login --username mnazaar

       1 -# Take-Home Assignment — Templated ID Generation Service       
       2 -                                                               
       3 -Thank you for interviewing with us. This is a **4-hour** assign
         -ment. When you hit 4 hours, stop and submit what you have — sco
         -ping to the time box is part of what we assess. AI coding tools
         - (Claude Code, Cursor, Copilot, etc.) are **required**, not jus
         -t permitted: how you drive them is part of the evaluation.     
       4 -                                                               
       5 -## The problem                                                 
       6 -                                                               
       7 -Government services issue human-readable, formatted identifiers
         - — receipt numbers, license numbers, complaint numbers — like `
         -PG-AMR-2026-27-000431`. Build a service that issues IDs from co
         -nfigurable templates.                                          
       8 -                                                               
       9 -**An occasional gap in the sequence is acceptable; a duplicate 
         -is never acceptable.**                                         
      10 -                                                               
      11 -Template definitions and the city master are provided in `data/
         -` (`templates.json`, `cities.csv`). Placeholder grammar:       
      12 -                                                               
      13 -| Placeholder | Meaning |                                      
      14 -|---|---|                                                      
      15 -| `[CITY]` | the requesting city's `city_code` |               
      16 -| `[FY:yyyy-yy]` / `[FY:yy-yy]` | current Indian financial year
         - (April–March), e.g. `2026-27` / `26-27` |                     
      17 -| `[CY:fmt]` | current date rendered with `fmt` (`yyyy`, `MM-yy
         -yy`, …) |                                                      
      18 -| `[SEQ:n]` | next counter value, zero-padded to `n` digits; co
         -unter is scoped per **(template, city)** |                     
      19 -| `[SEQ_NAME:n]` | like `[SEQ:n]` but uses the named counter `S
         -EQ_NAME` (scoped per city), whose configuration lives in the `c
         -ounters` section of `templates.json` |                         
      20 -                                                               
      21 -### Requirements                                               
      22 -                                                               
      23 -1. **Issue endpoint**: given `templateId`, `city` (tenant or ci
         -ty code), return one formatted ID.                             
      24 -2. **Bulk endpoint**: issue `count` IDs in one call.           
      25 -3. **Concurrency safety**: parallel requests — including bulk o
         -nes — must never produce a duplicate ID. **Your submission must
         - include a concurrency/load test that demonstrates this** (spaw
         -ning genuinely parallel workers against the running app).      
      26 -4. Requests referencing an unknown template, unknown city, or a
         - template whose named counter has no configuration must fail wi
         -th a clear, well-formed error.                                 
      27 -5. **Frontend**: a thin web UI — pick a template and city, ente
         -r a count, issue, and see the issued IDs (plus a simple view of
         - recently issued IDs). Plain HTML/JS is fine; function over pol
         -ish.                                                           
      28 -                                                               
      29 -## What to build (end to end)                                  
      30 -                                                               
      31 -- **Backend** (any language): API + persistence, loading `data/
         -` at startup.                                                  
      32 -- **Frontend**: as above.                                      
      33 -- **Containerization**: a `docker-compose.yml` at the repo root
         -. **`docker compose up` must be the only command needed** — bui
         -ld, start backend + frontend + database, load the template/city
         - data, and document the URL to open. No undocumented steps.    
      34 -                                                               
      35 -## Deliverables                                                
      36 -                                                               
      37 -1. `spec.md` — the problem restated; every ambiguity you found,
         - the decision you made, and why; what's explicitly out of scope
         -.                                                              
      38 -2. `plan.md` — milestones, data model, API sketch, test strateg
         -y, what you'd do with another day.                             
      39 -3. Working code (backend + frontend) + tests (including the con
         -currency test and how to run it) + `docker-compose.yml` + `READ
         -ME.md` with run instructions.                                  
      40 -4. `ai-log.md` — your prompts/transcript (or exported session),
         - plus 3–5 sentences on where the AI was wrong and how you caugh
         -t it.

  Decisions I made, each logged in docs/NOTES.md:

  │                                       │ format comes in step 3          │
  └───────────────────────────────────────┴─────────────────────────────────┘

  Where I depart from the brief's literal wording (all deliberate, all to be
  explained in spec.md):
  1. PGR-[SEQ:8] uses one global counter. The brief scopes [SEQ:n] per
     (template, city). Taken literally, every city would issue PGR-00000001, a
     duplicate the brief forbids.
  2. Counters reset when the printed period changes (new FY, year or month). The
     brief never mentions resets. Never resetting would also avoid duplicates,
     so this is a judgement call. It's already your #2 open question.
  3. The n in [SEQ:n] is a minimum width. The brief says "zero-padded to n
     digits". Values grow wider rather than being truncated, because truncation
     would wrap around into duplicates.

  I'm stricter than the brief in three places. The provided data passes all
  three, but you should know about them:
  - A template must have exactly one sequence placeholder.
  - City codes must be letters and digits only.
  - Named counters must be called SEQ_…. The brief writes [SEQ_NAME:n], and
    every counter in the data is SEQ_…, but a counter named something like
    WS_COUNTER would fail startup. That's arguably stricter than the brief
    means. I can loosen it so any [NAME:n] counts as a named counter. The cost
    is that a typo like [FOO:3] would then show up as a 422 at request time
    instead of failing startup.

  Risks the brief raises for later steps:
  - "API + persistence": the plan stores only Postgres sequences, with no tables
    (ADR-001). That's defensible, but a reviewer might expect issued IDs to be
    stored. The ADR answers it, so spec.md should say so plainly.
  - "Simple view of recently issued IDs": the plan keeps recent IDs in memory on
    each instance. Behind Nginx's round-robin, the UI's recent list would
    change on each refresh, depending on which of the 3 instances answers. That
    will look broken to a reviewer. Worth reconsidering before step 3; a small
    table, or reading recent IDs from all instances, would fix it.
  - Deliverables "present": the brief expects ai-log.md next to spec.md, plan.md
    and README.md, which reads as the repo root. We moved it to docs/. A short
    root ai-log.md that points to docs/ai-log.md avoids a failed checkbox.
  - 4-hour time box: the brief says to stop at 4 hours and submit, and scoping
    is assessed. Your plan's cut order already handles this.

  About docs/requirements/: you've added the brief and the data README there, so
  they'll go into the public repo when we commit. That's fine if it's intended.
  docs/requirements/README.md is a copy of data/README.md.

  Which of these do you want acted on now? I'd suggest:
  - loosening the SEQ_ naming rule;
  - deciding how the recent-IDs view should work before step 3;
  - adding a root ai-log.md pointer.

> give me postgres url

● Postgres isn't published to your machine. By design only Nginx's port 8080 is,
  so there's no localhost URL for it right now.

  Inside the Docker network (what the backends use):
  postgresql://idgen:idgen@postgres:5432/idgen
  That's user idgen, password idgen, database idgen, host postgres, port 5432.
  As JDBC: jdbc:postgresql://postgres:5432/idgen.

  To get a SQL prompt from your machine without opening a port:
  docker compose exec postgres psql -U idgen -d idgen
  Once there, \ds lists the sequences. There won't be any until step 3.

  To connect a GUI tool such as DBeaver or pgAdmin, Postgres needs a host port.
  I'd add a docker-compose.override.yml that you keep out of git:
  services:
    postgres:
      ports:
        - "5432:5432"
  After docker compose up -d, connect to
  postgresql://idgen:idgen@localhost:5432/idgen. Compose picks the override file
  up automatically, so the committed setup still exposes only Nginx, as the
  README check and ADR-004 say. Use 5433:5432 if something on your machine
  already uses 5432.

  Want me to add the override file and gitignore it?

> select * from all_sequences;

● all_sequences is an Oracle view; Postgres doesn't have it. The Postgres
  equivalent is pg_sequences:

  select * from pg_sequences;

  Or, to see just the columns that matter for this design:

  select sequencename, start_value, increment_by, last_value
  from pg_sequences
  order by sequencename;

  information_schema.sequences is the SQL-standard view, but it has no
  last_value.

  Running it now shows the result:

  Ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

● It returns 0 rows, as expected: nothing creates sequences until step 3
  (SequenceStore).

  After step 3 you'll see one row per counter key, such as
  seq_pg_receipt_amr_2026_27. increment_by is the block size, 100 (or 10 for
  narrow counters like SEQ_WS). last_value is the start of the most recent block
  an instance took, and it stays empty until the first nextval.

> i dont see the sequence in db

● That's expected: nothing has created any sequences yet. Steps 1 and 2 don't
  touch the database beyond the readiness check. Step 2 only reads
  templates.json and cities.csv and works out each counter key in memory, such
  as PG_RECEIPT | AMR | 2026-27.

  Sequences arrive in step 3, with SequenceStore and BlockAllocator:
  - At startup, each instance runs CREATE SEQUENCE IF NOT EXISTS … for every
    template × city in the current period. For example,
    seq_pg_receipt_amr_2026_27 for receipts and seq_pgr_complaint for
    complaints, which have one global counter.
  - On the first ID for a key, the instance calls nextval and takes a block of
    100 numbers. last_value in pg_sequences is set from then on.

  So after step 3, select * from pg_sequences will show around 50 rows: 5
  issuable templates × 10 cities, with PGR as a single global row. EST_ALLOTMENT
  gets none, because its counter isn't configured.

  Step 2's changes are still staged and waiting for your review. Do you want to
  review and commit those first, or should I start step 3 on top of them?

> how to test step 2 manually

● Most of step 2 is internal (parsing, rendering, counter keys), and IDs can't
  be issued over HTTP until step 3. So the manual checks are the two endpoints,
  readiness, startup errors on bad config, and the tests. Before I give you the
  startup-error check, I'll confirm it actually works:

  Ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

● It works: the bad template stops startup with a clear message naming the file,
  template and position. Here are the steps. They're written for PowerShell, so
  they use curl.exe.

  1. Rebuild with the step 2 code
  docker compose up --build -d
  docker compose logs backend | Select-String "Loaded|EST_ALLOTMENT"
  Each of the 3 backends should log Loaded 6 templates and 10 cities, followed
  by a warning that EST_ALLOTMENT's counter has no configuration.

  2. Templates endpoint
  curl.exe -s http://localhost:8080/api/templates
  Check for:
  - 6 templates, in the same order as in the file.
  - PGR_COMPLAINT with "scope": "counter": one global counter, with no city or
    period.
  - WS_CONNECTION with "counter": "SEQ_WS".
  - EST_ALLOTMENT with "canIssue": false and "reason": "Counter SEQ_EST_ALLOT
    has no configuration".

  You can also open http://localhost:8080/api/templates in a browser to see the
  JSON formatted.

  3. Cities endpoint
  curl.exe -s http://localhost:8080/api/cities
  You should get 10 cities, from pb.amritsar / AMR to tn.madurai / MDU.

  4. Readiness includes the loaded config
  curl.exe -s http://localhost:8080/q/health/ready
  "Template and city configuration" should be UP with templates: 6, cities: 10,
  next to the Postgres check.

  5. Bad config stops startup with a clear message

  This uses a copy of the data, so data/ itself is never touched:
  New-Item -ItemType Directory -Force C:\temp\idgen-bad | Out-Null
  Copy-Item data\* C:\temp\idgen-bad\
  (Get-Content C:\temp\idgen-bad\templates.json) -replace
  '\[SEQ:8\]','[BOGUS]-[SEQ:8]' | Set-Content C:\temp\idgen-bad\templates.json
  docker compose run --rm --no-deps -v C:/temp/idgen-bad:/data:ro backend
  Expect:
  ConfigException: /data/templates.json: Template PGR_COMPLAINT, position 5:
  unknown placeholder [BOGUS]
  You can try other mistakes in the copy:
  - Remove [SEQ:8]: "no [SEQ:n] … placeholder, so every ID would be the same".
  - Break the JSON: the error names the line and column.
  - Duplicate a city code in cities.csv: "'AMR' already used on line 2".

  Clean up afterwards with Remove-Item -Recurse C:\temp\idgen-bad.

  6. Rendering, via the tests

  You can't see rendered IDs over HTTP until step 3, so the tests are where to
  check them:
  .\mvnw.cmd test "-Dtest=RenderTest,TemplateParserTest,DataLoaderTest"
  The test names describe what each checks:
  - firstIdForAmritsar: the five issuable templates' first IDs.
  - financialYearTurnsOnFirstOfApril.
  - paddingIsAMinimumWidthAndValuesGrowPastIt.
  - pgrHasOneGlobalKeyBecauseItsIdsPrintNoCity.
  - estAllotmentIsRejectedBecauseItsCounterHasNoConfiguration.

  To try your own cases, edit the values in RenderTest.

  Want me to add steps 2–5 to the README's manual testing section?

> give me command to restart teh pods

● These are Docker Compose containers, not Kubernetes pods, so use docker
  compose:

  Restart all 3 backends (for example, to reload data/ after a change):
  docker compose restart backend

  Restart a single backend:
  docker restart idgen-backend-2

  Restart everything (Postgres, backends, Nginx):
  docker compose restart

  Rebuild and restart after a code change. A plain restart keeps the old image,
  so code changes need a rebuild:
  docker compose up --build -d

  Check they came back healthy:
  docker compose ps

  A restart keeps each container's IP address, so Nginx keeps routing without a
  gap. A rebuild recreates the containers, which can give a few seconds of 502s
  until Nginx picks up the new addresses.

  You have data/cities.csv open. Editing it is fine for experiments, since a
  restart reloads it, but put it back before committing. The brief requires the
  files byte-for-byte as delivered, and git diff data/ will show any change.

● Step 3 is done: single and bulk issuing work for every template × city, and
  every error code returns the documented shape. I haven't committed anything;
  it's ready for your review.

  What works:
  - All 206 tests pass. They cover the 50 template × city pairs single and bulk,
    all error codes, a 1,000-ID bulk spanning 100 blocks, and parallel requests
    with no duplicates.
  - Real stack, 3 instances behind Nginx:
    - Each instance takes its own block of numbers: repeated calls return
      PG-AMR-2026-27-000001, …000101, …000201, then …000002.
    - 2,000 parallel requests returned 22,564 IDs with 0 duplicates.
    - All 41 sequences are created at startup: 4 templates × 10 cities plus one
      global PGR sequence, and none for EST. Water connection uses blocks of 10.
    - With Postgres stopped, counters that still have numbers in memory keep
      issuing. Others return 503 SEQUENCE_UNAVAILABLE and recover once Postgres
      is back, with no restart.

  What changed:

  Area: Sequences (sequence/)
  Files: SequenceStore: the only code that talks to Postgres. BlockAllocator:
  one
    lock per sequence; a bulk fetches all the blocks it needs in one trip. Plus
    SequenceNames, SequenceSpec, and SequenceWarmup, which creates the sequences

    at startup
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Area: Issuing
  Files: issue/IdService, recent/RecentIds
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Area: API
  Files: IdResource: POST /api/ids, POST /api/ids/bulk, GET /api/ids/recent.
    ErrorMapper + ErrorBody: one error shape everywhere; a 500 carries a
    correlation ID
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Area: Tests
  Files: IdResourceTest, BlockAllocatorTest (fake store, 32 threads),
    SequenceNamesTest, ErrorMapperTest

  A bug the tests missed: in Docker, the startup step that creates sequences ran
  before the config had loaded, so it created none. I'd assumed injecting the
  loader makes it load first, but Quarkus injects it lazily. I only caught this
  by checking pg_sequences on the real stack; it's fixed, and it's in the AI log
  as a "where the AI was wrong" example. To see the startup creation from a
  clean slate, I wiped your local database's public schema. It only held the 2
  test sequences from a minute earlier.

  Decisions for your review (also in docs/NOTES.md):
  - Request checks run before lookups. So count: 0 for an unknown template
    returns INVALID_COUNT, not UNKNOWN_TEMPLATE.
  - count must be a JSON integer. "5", 2.5 and true all return INVALID_COUNT.
  - Three error codes not in the spec's table: NOT_FOUND, METHOD_NOT_ALLOWED and
    UNSUPPORTED_MEDIA_TYPE, for the framework's own 404/405/415. They use the
    same shape and should be added to the API contract.
  - Sequences are created at startup for the current period only. The ADR says
    current and next; next period's sequences are created on first use.

  Still open, needed before the UI step: recent IDs are kept per instance, as
  the spec says. Behind Nginx's round-robin, the UI's "recent" list will change
  with whichever of the 3 instances answers. Options:
  - accept it and say so in the UI;
  - have Nginx send each browser to the same instance;
  - or store recent IDs in a small Postgres table.

  The README's manual testing now covers issuing, the errors, sequences in
  Postgres, and the Postgres-down behaviour. Say "commit" when you're happy, or
  tell me what to change.

● Adding a city works without code changes, as long as the backends are
  restarted. I tested it by adding ka.hubli,HBL,Hubli to a copy of cities.csv
  and starting one extra backend with that copy:

  - After a restart, the city works straight away. The backend logged Loaded 6
    templates and 11 cities and Sequences ready: 45, which is the existing 41
    plus 4 for Hubli. It then issued PG-HBL-2026-27-000001, and a bulk call
    returned WS-HBL-1000-11-2026 and WS-HBL-1001-11-2026.
  - Its sequences are created automatically: seq_pg_receipt_hbl_2026_27,
    seq_tl_license_hbl_2026, seq_ws_hbl_11_2026 and seq_bpa_permit_hbl_26_27. If
    Postgres were down at that moment, they'd be created on the first request
    instead.
  - Complaint numbers carry on from the one global counter. They don't restart
    at 1 for the new city.
  - Without a restart, the city is unknown. The 3 running instances still
    returned 404 UNKNOWN_CITY for ka.hubli, because the files are only read at
    startup.

  Things to watch:
  - Restart every instance. During a one-by-one restart, an updated instance
    accepts the new city while an old one returns 404 for it. That's harmless
    for uniqueness but inconsistent for a few seconds.
  - A bad row stops startup with a clear message. That covers a duplicate code,
    a code with - or /, or a missing field. It's better than starting with half
    the cities.
  - For the submission, leave the real data/cities.csv alone. The brief requires
    the provided files unchanged. I used a copy, and the test backend and copy
    are now removed. The 4 hbl sequences are still in your local database;
    they're harmless.

  ADR-003 says the same thing: "Onboarding a city = a row in cities.csv. Its
  sequences are created at the next startup, or on first use." If the city list
  had to change without restarts, it would belong in a master-data service
  (ADR-003's "another day" note).

● I've added ADR-005: Templates and cities are loaded once at startup; changes
  need a restart. It's in your Claude Doc after ADR-004, in
  docs/architecture.md, and in docs/adr/0005-config-loaded-at-startup.md.

  What it says:
  - The decision: both files are read once, validated, and held in memory, with
    templates already parsed. Any change needs every backend restarted. That's a
    restart only, not a rebuild, because data/ is mounted into the containers.
  - Evidence: the Hubli test from earlier. After a restart, 4 new sequences were
    created and PG-HBL-2026-27-000001 was issued; instances not yet restarted
    answered 404.
  - A table of which changes are safe:
    - Safe: adding a city, a template or a named counter.
    - Removing a city or template is safe for uniqueness: its sequences stay, so
      re-adding it continues the numbering.
    - Changing a counter's start after it has issued IDs has no effect.
  - Consequences:
    - A restart is needed to apply changes.
    - During a one-by-one restart, instances disagree about the new city for a
      few seconds.
    - A bad file stops startup rather than letting the service run on half a
      configuration.
  - With another day:
    - Reload without a restart: validate fully, then swap in the new config in
      one step. The registries are already built to be replaced whole.
    - A guard that refuses risky format changes.
    - A reload event so all replicas switch together.
    - Long term, a master-data service such as DIGIT's MDMS.

  A risk I found while writing it: editing an existing template's format isn't
  safe in general. If the edit changes what its counter is scoped by, for
  example removing [CITY], a new sequence starts from 1 and can re-issue IDs
  that were already printed under the old format. The ADR marks this as the one
  unsafe change: a changed format should get a new template ID. This wasn't
  covered anywhere in the spec before, so it may be worth raising in the
  interview.

  Also updated:
  - "0001–0004" now reads "0001–0005" in the doc, the local copy, CLAUDE.md and
    the repo layout.
  - docs/ai-log.md and docs/NOTES.md have entries for this.
  - The decisions list in REMINDERS.md has a line for it.

  This and step 3 are both uncommitted, waiting for your review.

      250 -## ADR-001: Postgres sequences with in-memory blocks, no table
          -s                                                             
      250 +## Architecture decision records                              
      251  
      252 -**Decision:** each counter key gets one Postgres sequence with
          - `INCREMENT BY 100`. A backend instance calls `nextval` once, 
          -takes the 100 numbers starting there, and issues them from mem
          -ory. The service has no tables. Accepted, 13 Nov 2026.        
      252 +Each decision lives in its own file under [`docs/adr/`](adr/):
      253  
      254 -**Context.** If every ID needs a DB round trip, busy keys beco
          -me a bottleneck. The brief allows gaps but never a duplicate, 
          -and that is how Postgres sequences behave: `nextval` never blo
          -cks other callers, never rolls back, and never returns the sam
          -e value twice. You can get gaps, but never duplicates.        
      255 -                                                              
      256 -**How it works**                                              
      257 -1. **One sequence per counter key:** (template, city, period) 
          -for `[SEQ:n]`; (named counter, city, period) for `[SEQ_NAME:n]
          -`. Templates with no `[CITY]` in the output (`PGR-`) get one g
          -lobal sequence. Names follow `seq_<counter>_<city>_<period>` i
          -n lower case, within Postgres's 63-character limit.           
      258 -1. **Created at startup** for the current and next period, `ST
          -ART WITH` the configured `start` (1 by default). If another in
          -stance creates it at the same moment, catch `duplicate_object`
          - and carry on.                                                
      259 -1. **In memory:** `{next, end}` per key, guarded by a per-key 
          -lock. When `next == end`, one `nextval` returns `base`, and th
          -e block is `[base, base+100)`. Only one refill per key runs at
          - a time; other callers wait for it.                           
      260 -1. **Bulk:** take what's left in the block. If more are needed
          -, fetch several blocks in one trip with `SELECT nextval(…) FRO
          -M generate_series(1, k)`.                                     
      261 -1. **Recent-IDs view:** an in-memory list of the last 50 IDs p
          -er (template, city) on each instance.                         
      262 -                                                              
      263 -```sql                                                        
      264 -CREATE SEQUENCE IF NOT EXISTS seq_pg_receipt_amr_2026_27 START
          - WITH 1    INCREMENT BY 100;                                  
      265 -CREATE SEQUENCE IF NOT EXISTS seq_ws_amr                 START
          - WITH 1000 INCREMENT BY 10;                                   
      266 -CREATE SEQUENCE IF NOT EXISTS seq_pgr_complaint          START
          - WITH 1    INCREMENT BY 100;                                  
      267 -                                                              
      268 -SELECT nextval('seq_pg_receipt_amr_2026_27');                 
          -      -- one block                                            
      269 -SELECT nextval('seq_pg_receipt_amr_2026_27') FROM generate_ser
          -ies(1, 5);  -- five blocks, one trip                          
      270 -```                                                           
      271 -                                                              
      272 -**Why it can't duplicate.** Each `nextval` returns a different
          - starting number, `INCREMENT` apart, so blocks never overlap, 
          -whether across instances, restarts or crashes. The in-memory l
          -ock only protects a block that this one instance already owns.
      273 -                                                              
      274 -**Why no tables at all.** The only state that has to be durabl
          -e and shared is "which numbers are taken", and a sequence hold
          -s exactly that. Templates and cities are configuration loaded 
          -from `data/` at startup, not data the service owns. The recent
          --IDs view is a convenience and can live in memory. Any table w
          -ould either duplicate what the sequence already guarantees (a 
          -counters table) or put a write back on every request (an issue
          -d-IDs log). If an audit trail is needed later, add an `issued_
          -ids` table written in batches off the request path. That is an
          - additive change and doesn't affect how numbers are allocated.
      275 -                                                              
      276 -| Option | DB trips per ID | Tables | Verdict |               
      277 -|---|---|---|---|                                             
      278 -| Counters table, `UPDATE` per request | 1 | 1 | Hot row lock;
          - most moving parts |                                          
      279 -| Counters table, hi-lo block | 1/100 | 1 | Works, but rebuild
          -s what a sequence already is |                                
      280 -| Sequence with `CACHE 100` | 1 | 0 | Caches inside each Postg
          -res connection; the app still makes a trip per `nextval` |    
      281 -| **Sequence ****`INCREMENT BY 100`**** + app block (chosen)**
          - | 1/100 | 0 | Fewest trips, no locks in the DB, nothing to ma
          -intain |                                                      
      282 -                                                              
      283 -**Consequences**                                              
      284 -- **Gaps:** an instance restart or a new period leaves up to 9
          -9 unused numbers per key per instance. The brief allows this.

      16 +                                                               
      17 +```sh

         +eady running                                                   
      19 +docker compose --profile test run --rm loadtest    # 50 process
         +es, 30 s

      21 +                                                               
      22 +[`loadtest/loadtest.py`](loadtest/loadtest.py) starts **50 genu
         +inely parallel OS processes** (`multiprocessing`, not threads).
         + Each loops for 30 s over random templates and cities (tenant I
         +Ds, codes, mixed case), sending single requests and bulk reques
         +ts of 1–50 through Nginx to all 3 backends. It collects every I
         +D returned and **exits 1 if any ID appears twice**. EST_ALLOTME
         +NT is included on purpose: it must keep returning 422 under loa
         +d. Options: `-e DURATION=60 -e WORKERS=100`. A JSON summary is 
         +written to `loadtest/out/`.                                    
      23 +                                                               
      24 +Result on a laptop (3 backends, Postgres 16):                  
      25 +                                                               
      26 +```

      30 +DUPLICATES            0

      33 +By prefix     {'PGR': 22344, 'WS': 22217, 'TL': 21953, 'BPA': 2
         +1913, 'PG': 21749}

         +, '172.18.0.5:8080': 4445}

      36 +PASS: 110,176 IDs, 0 duplicates

         +hen restart the backends from a second terminal while it runs: 
      40 +

      43 +docker compose restart backend                     # second ter
         +minal, ~10 s in

  What's new:
  - loadtest/loadtest.py: 50 real OS processes sending random template × city
    requests, single and bulk (1–50). It collects every ID and fails on any
    duplicate. Errors are counted but don't fail the run. It uses only the
    Python standard library, so the container installs nothing. Run it with
    docker compose --profile test run --rm loadtest.
  - loadtest/idgen.jmx: the JMeter plan. It checks each response is HTTP 200
    with an ID matching its template's pattern. Its requests come from
    requests.csv (loadtest/jmeter/requests.csv), which make_requests.py
    (loadtest/jmeter/make_requests.py) generates. JMeter runs from a Dockerfile
    (loadtest/jmeter/Dockerfile) that installs it from the Apache archive with a
    SHA-512 check, rather than a third-party image. Run it with docker compose
    --profile jmeter run --rm jmeter; the HTML report goes to
    loadtest/out/jmeter/report/.
  - docker-compose.yml: the new jmeter service.
  - The README, NOTES and AI log are updated with this step and its results.

  Things that went wrong along the way (all in the AI log):
  - Invalid .jmx file: XML comments can't contain --, so my header comment with
    --profile stopped JMeter from loading it.
  - Every check failed on good responses: the CSV regexes contained commas,
    which split the CSV columns, so every assertion failed even though the
    responses were HTTP 200. The CSV is now generated by a script with patterns
    that contain no commas.
  - docker compose build -q didn't rebuild the image: the same trap as in
    step 2.
  - One unexplained trial: one JMeter trial got 502s right after compose
    restarted Nginx. It didn't happen again in five later runs.

  Two suggestions, not done:
  1. Return JSON while no backend is up. When all backends are down, Nginx
     returns its own HTML 502 page instead of our JSON error shape. Reviewers
     "hammering endpoints" could hit this during a restart. A few lines in
     nginx.conf (error_page 502 503 504 returning
     {"error":{"code":"SERVICE_UNAVAILABLE",…}}) would make every error
     well-formed JSON, as the brief asks.
  2. Restart one backend at a time. Doing that during the load test should give
     close to zero errors, with Nginx routing around the instance being
     restarted. It's a stronger demo than restarting all three at once.

  Say "commit" when you're happy, or tell me what to change.

> currently the recently issued ids are retrieving from individual memories. if
  one more day is there, we can put that as a centralized cache using redis or
  similar caching services instead of indiviual pods. however the  existing
  way of caching the ids locally and reducing round trip is intentional here,
  and redis will not be used because that will increase network latency to go
  to redis. it is acceptable here that the ids are not issues exactly in
  sequnce and jump based on pod it goes. document this in read me and adr as
  necessary

● I'll write this up as ADR-006 and add a short README section that reviewers
  will see before they start testing. Both behaviours come from the same choice,
  keeping per-instance state in memory, so they belong in one ADR.

● Write(docs\adr\0006-per-instance-memory-no-redis.md)
  ⎿  Wrote 36 lines to docs\adr\0006-per-instance-memory-no-redis.md
      1 ## ADR-006: Number blocks and recent IDs stay in each instance's
        memory (no Redis)
      2
      3 **Decision:** each backend instance keeps two things in its own m
        emory: its current block of numbers per counter (ADR-001) and its
         last 50 issued IDs per (template, city). Neither goes through Re
        dis or any other central cache. We accept that IDs are not issued
         in strict sequence across instances, and that the recent-IDs vie
        w shows only what the instance answering has issued. Accepted, 13
         Nov 2026.
     … +33 lines (ctrl+o to expand)
