  ⎿  Updated README.md (+10 -1)
      12  Then open http://localhost:8080 (`/api/ping`, `/q/health/ready`
          ).
      13  The first build downloads Maven dependencies and takes a few mi
          nutes.
      14  
      15 +## How numbering behaves (by design)                           
      16 +                                                               
      17 +- **Unique, not sequential.** Each of the 3 backend instances t
         +akes its own block of numbers from a Postgres sequence (100 at 
         +a time, 10 for narrow counters) and issues from memory. Request
         +s are spread round-robin, so consecutive IDs come from differen
         +t blocks: `PG-AMR-2026-27-000001`, `…000101`, `…000201`, `…0000
         +02`. Within one instance they count up; across instances they i
         +nterleave. Use `issuedAt`, not the ID, for issue order.        
      18 +- **Gaps are expected.** A restarted instance abandons the rest
         + of its block. The brief allows gaps; it never allows duplicate
         +s, and blocks never overlap.                                   
      19 +- **No Redis on purpose.** A shared counter would add a network
         + round trip to every ID; local blocks need one Postgres call pe
         +r 100 IDs (817 req/s at p95 56 ms below).                      
      20 +- **"Recent IDs" is per instance.** `GET /api/ids/recent` shows
         + what the instance that answers has issued, so a refresh can sh
         +ow another instance's list, and a restart empties it. A shared 
         +view (Redis, written asynchronously so issuing isn't slowed) is
         + the "another day" item.                                       
      21 +                                                               
      22 +Details and alternatives: [ADR-001](docs/adr/0001-sequences-in-
         +memory-blocks.md), [ADR-006](docs/adr/0006-per-instance-memory-
         +no-redis.md).                                                  
      23 +                                                               
      24  ## Concurrency test (zero duplicates)
      25  
      26  ```sh
     ...
      116  curl -X POST http://localhost:8080/api/ids/bulk -H "Content-Ty
           pe: application/json"      -d '{"templateId":"WS_CONNECTION","
           city":"LDH","count":3}'
      117  curl "http://localhost:8080/api/ids/recent?templateId=PG_RECEI
           PT&city=AMR&limit=10"
      118  ```
      110 -Repeat the first call: each of the 3 instances takes its own b
          -lock of 100, so you'll see `…-000001`, `…-000101`, `…-000201`,
          - then `…-000002`. Unique, with gaps by design (ADR-001).      
      119 +Repeat the first call: each of the 3 instances takes its own b
          +lock of 100, so you'll see `…-000001`, `…-000101`, `…-000201`,
          + then `…-000002`. Unique, with gaps by design (see [How number
          +ing behaves](#how-numbering-behaves-by-design)). The recent li
          +st can also change between calls: it is per instance.         
      120

● I've documented your decision as ADR-006: Number blocks and recent IDs stay in
  each instance's memory (no Redis), and added a short section near the top of
  the README. It's ready for review and not committed yet.

  ADR-006 (file (docs/adr/0006-per-instance-memory-no-redis.md), plus your
  Claude Doc after ADR-005):
  - Decision: each instance keeps its own number blocks and its own last 50 IDs
    in memory. Nothing goes through Redis. We accept that IDs aren't strictly
    sequential and that the recent view is per instance.
  - Context: the real sequence from step 3: 000001 (A), 000101 (B), 000201 (C),
    000002 (A again). It quotes the brief: gaps are acceptable, duplicates
    never.
  - Why numbers stay local:
    - A Redis INCR would add a network hop to every ID; local blocks need one
      Postgres call per 100 IDs.
    - Redis would be a second stateful store, and if its durability settings
      ever let a counter go backwards, that means duplicates.
    - It cites the measured 817 req/s at p95 56 ms.
  - Options compared for the recent view: per-instance memory (chosen), a Redis
    list, a Postgres table, and Nginx sticky sessions.
  - Consequences:
    - IDs interleave across instances; use issuedAt for issue order.
    - There are gaps after restarts.
    - The recent list depends on which instance answers, and the UI labels it as
      such.
    - No extra infrastructure is needed.
  - Another day: publish issued IDs to a Redis list asynchronously, so issuing
    never waits for Redis, and Redis is never on the path that decides a number.

  README: a new section, "How numbering behaves (by design)", sits right after
  "Run", so reviewers read it before they test. It has four points: unique but
  not sequential, gaps are expected, no Redis on purpose, and the recent list is
  per instance. Each links to ADR-001 and ADR-006. The manual-testing step
  about repeated calls now links to it and mentions the recent list.

  Also updated:
  - "0001–0005" now reads "0001–0006" in the Claude Doc, the local spec and
    CLAUDE.md, and both repo layouts list the new file.
  - The ADR link list in docs/architecture.md has ADR-006.
  - docs/NOTES.md marks the recent-IDs question as resolved.
  - The AI log has an entry for this.
  - The decisions list in REMINDERS.md mentions it.

  For the UI step: the ADR promises that the recent panel will say "recently
  issued on this instance". I'll build it that way.

  Say "commit" when you're happy.

> commit and push

> 6:40–7:00 UI. index.html: template + city dropdowns from the meta endpoints,
  a count field, issue, a results list, and a recent-IDs panel.

● Building the UI as one static page,
  src/main/resources/META-INF/resources/index.html. Quarkus serves it at /, and
  through Nginx it's on the same origin as the API, so no CORS setup is needed.
  It uses plain HTML and JS with no external libraries, so nothing has to
  download in a reviewer's browser.

  Design choices, following the spec and ADR-006:
  - Dropdowns come from /api/templates and /api/cities. EST_ALLOTMENT stays
    selectable but is marked "counter not configured", so reviewers can see the
    422 error rendered.
  - Count = 1 uses POST /api/ids; more than 1 uses /api/ids/bulk.
  - Results list every batch issued from this page, newest first, with which
    instance served it (Nginx's X-Upstream header) and a copy button.
  - The recent panel is labelled as the answering instance's list, filterable by
    the current selection, and refreshes after each issue.
  - Errors show the API's code and message.

  What's there: a single page with no libraries and no build step, index.html
  (src/main/resources/META-INF/resources/index.html), served by Quarkus at /.
  - Template and city dropdowns come from the API. EST_ALLOTMENT is marked
    "counter not configured" but stays selectable, so you can see the 422 error.
  - Count: 1 issues a single ID, 2–1000 issues a bulk.
  - "Issued from this page" lists each batch, newest first, with its template,
    city, time, the instance that served it, and a copy button.
  - "Recently issued on this instance" names the answering instance and has a
    filter toggle and a Refresh button. An empty list explains that requests
    rotate between instances, as ADR-006 says.
  - Errors show the API's code, message and details. A count outside 1–1000 is
    caught in the browser.
  - Layout: light and dark themes, and a single column on narrow screens.

● I added a "Testing with the UI" section to the README (README.md), right after
  "How numbering behaves", so reviewers see it before the Postman and curl
  sections. It's a 10-step table, each step with what to do and what you should
  see:

  1. Issue a single receipt ID.
  2. Issue a few more and watch IDs from different instances interleave.
  3. Bulk-issue 20 water-connection IDs and copy them.
  4. Issue PGR for two cities: there's no city in the ID, and the counter
     doesn't restart for the second city.
  5. Select EST_ALLOTMENT and read the warning hint.
  6. Issue EST_ALLOTMENT and see the 422 error box.
  7. Enter a count of 0 or 1001: rejected in the browser.
  8. Refresh the recent panel and watch it change with the instance (ADR-006).
  9. Stop all backends: SERVICE_UNAVAILABLE in the same error box, then
     recovery.
  10. Stop Postgres: SEQUENCE_UNAVAILABLE for a combination you haven't used
      yet, while used ones keep issuing.

  I tightened two rows after drafting them, because they promised more than the
  system guarantees:
  - Example numbers like 000001 or WS-LDH-1000 only appear on a fresh database.
  - PGR numbers don't necessarily continue exactly from Amritsar to Chennai,
    since another instance may answer. What's guaranteed is that Chennai doesn't
    restart at 1.

  Steps 9 and 10 depend on behaviour I checked earlier with curl; the UI shows
  the same JSON error.

  I've logged this in the AI log. Not committed: the UI step, the Postman docs
  and this section are all ready for review together.

● Nginx alternates strictly per request across all three backends (10 of 30
  each), so it isn't pinning them. I'm checking the Nginx log next to see
  whether your browser's requests actually go through this server. · summary

● The logs show your browser's requests go through Nginx and are spread over the
  instances. Look at the response sizes for your Refresh clicks on "PG_RECEIPT
  / Bengaluru":

  GET /api/ids/recent?...city=ka.bengaluru   200   2      ← empty list []
  GET /api/ids/recent?...city=ka.bengaluru   200   4171   ← the instance that
  issued your bulk
  GET /api/ids/recent?...city=ka.bengaluru   200   2
  GET /api/ids/recent?...city=ka.bengaluru   200   2
  GET /api/ids/recent?...city=ka.bengaluru   200   4171
  …

  About one Refresh in three returns your IDs and the other two return an empty
  list. That's round-robin over 3 instances, where only the instance that served
  your bulk issue has those IDs in memory (ADR-006).

  Other checks:
  - Nginx rotates on every request, even on one kept-alive connection like a
    browser's: .3 → .5 → .4 → .3 …, 10 each out of 30.
  - DNS returns all 3 backends to Nginx.
  - Your browser does reach Nginx: the requests above carry your Chrome user
    agent.

  How to see it in the UI:
  - In "Recently issued on this instance", press Refresh several times and watch
    "(this time: 172.18.0.x:8080)" cycle through three addresses. The list
    fills on one and is empty on the other two.
  - Each batch under "Issued from this page" shows the instance that served it.
    Issue a few times and the addresses vary.
  - In DevTools (F12) → Network, click any /api/... request; the X-Upstream
    response header names the instance.

  If you still see the same address every time, tell me where you're looking
  (the batch labels or the "this time" label) and what you clicked between
  requests. One pattern can look sticky: the page sends two requests per Issue
  click (issue, then refresh recent), so the instance sequence for your clicks
  is less obvious than with curl.

      29  ## How numbering behaves (by design)
      30  
      31  - **Unique, not sequential.** Each of the 3 backend instances t
          akes its own block of numbers from a Postgres sequence (100 at 
          a time, 10 for narrow counters) and issues from memory. Request
          s are spread round-robin, so consecutive IDs come from differen
          t blocks: `PG-AMR-2026-27-000001`, `…000101`, `…000201`, `…0000
          02`. Within one instance they count up; across instances they i
          nterleave. Use `issuedAt`, not the ID, for issue order.
